### Step-by-Step Reasoning:

1. **Identify Direct Connections:**
   - The target nodes are D and E.
   - There is no direct edge between D and E.

2. **Find Paths from D to E:**
   - Paths must be identified through intermediary nodes.

   **Paths from D to E:**
   a. D → J → P → I → C → N → B → H → G → K → F → E
   b. D → J → P → I → C → O → Y → N → B → H → G → K → F → E

3. **Isolate Critical Edges:**
   - To disconnect D from E, we need to cut all the intermediary paths.
   - There are multiple paths from D to E, and they all must be disrupted simultaneously.

4. **Minimum Cut Set:**
   - We need to ensure that removing the least number of edges breaks all possible paths between D and E.
   - We must select edges such that removing them prevents any alternate path through other intermediary nodes.

   **Edges to Remove:**
   - Removing (D, J) cuts off the only connection from D.
   - Checking possible alternative paths identifies (N, B) as another critical point, where multiple paths converge.

   **Evaluate Edge Removal:**
   - Removing (D, J) and (N, B) should sufficiently disconnect all paths.

### Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(D, J), (N, B)]

To determine the minimum number of edges that need to be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes \(X\) and \(C\), we need to analyze the graph and identify the paths connecting them.

### Step-by-Step Reasoning:

1. **Identify Paths between \(X\) and \(C\):**
   - \(X\) to \(C\): Direct path. Remove \((X, C)\).
   - Alternative paths (Paths \(X\) to \(C\)):
     - \(X \to K \to J \to P \to I \to H \to G \to B \to N \to Y \to O \to C\).
     - \(X \to K \to J \to P \to I \to H \to G \to E \to L \to M \to F \to H \to G \to B \to N \to Y \to O \to C\).
     - \(X \to K \to J \to P \to I \to H \to G \to E \to L \to M \to C\).

2. **Minimize Edge Removal:**
   - Removing the edge \((X, C)\) directly cuts the direct path.
   - To cut the alternative paths efficiently:
     - Removing edge \((K, J)\) will block several paths.
     - Removing edge \((G, B)\) or \((N, Y)\) or \((O, C)\) will help block alternative paths effectively.
   - This won't suffice to fully disconnect \(X\) and \(C\); thus, another edge needs to be removed either \((H, G)\) or \((P, I)\).

3. **Strategy:**
   - After removing the edge \((X, C)\), further breaks needed in critical connection to eliminate the alternative paths:
     - To isolate \(X\) and \(C\) totally check paths through neighboring nodes:
       - Removing \((H, G)\).
       - Removing \((P, I)\) ensures no possible indirect paths remain.

Final verification:
- We remove edges \((X, C)\), \((K, J)\), and \((G, B)\).
- All possible paths via \(P, I\) are also blocked.

### Conclusion:

To ensure that there is no possible path between \(X\) and \(C\), we need to remove the following edges:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(X, C), (K, J), (G, B)].

   We need to explore all potential paths connecting \(B\) and \(H\). Below is the list of possible paths:

   - Direct Path:
     - \(B \rightarrow E \rightarrow P \rightarrow H\)
     - \(B \rightarrow E \rightarrow J \rightarrow H\)
   - Indirect Path 1:
     - \(B \rightarrow E \rightarrow J \rightarrow F \rightarrow H\)
   - Indirect Path 2:
     - \(B \rightarrow C \rightarrow I \rightarrow H\)
   - Indirect Path 3:
     - \(B \rightarrow O \rightarrow P \rightarrow H\)
   - Indirect Path 4:
     - \(B \rightarrow E \rightarrow J \rightarrow K \rightarrow H\)

3. **Identify Critical Edges:**
   To disconnect \(B\) from \(H\), each of these paths must be broken. We need to cut the minimal set of edges to ensurenone of these paths exist.

4. **Validating Cut Set Candidates:**
   - If we remove the edge \((E, P)\):
     - Path 1 is broken \((B \rightarrow E \rightarrow P \rightarrow H)\).
   - Removing the edge \((J, F)\) will break:
     - Path 2 \((B \rightarrow E \rightarrow J \rightarrow F \rightarrow H)\).

     - Path 3 \((B \rightarrow C \rightarrow I \rightarrow H)\).

   - Removing the edge \((J, K)\) will break:
     - Path 5 \((B \rightarrow E \rightarrow J \rightarrow K \rightarrow H)\).

However, the above step is not efficient because there are potentially overlapping paths and removing multiple edges ends up breaking other paths simultaneously. The minimal cut that maximally disrupts paths will be observed when the identified nodes and paths overlap in our analysis. Therefore, each path must be adequately severed individually, keeping intersecting points in view.

5. **Systematic Removal to Ensure Minimal Edges:**

   - Remove edges to isolate H from others:
     - \((B, E)\) blocks paths involving node \(E\)
     - \((E, P)\) or \((J, P)\) to isolate \(H\)
     - \((F, H)\) disrupts paths involving F's adjacency
     - \((I, H)\) or \((O, P)\) disrupts other adjacency routes

Since removing a combination of considered nodes severs required paths without redundant cuts, the minimal necessary should be checked. The detailed scrutinization shows removing side connections disrupts all paths via critical edge severance ensuring disrupted routes.

### Balancing Minimal Cuts:
- Analyzing combinations from paths between B and H above ensures seeing minimal constraints.

### Final Answer:
   To completely disconnect nodes B and H, the minimal number of edges that must be removed is 3.

   - Paths can be grouped as follows:
     - Group 1: Paths passing through I (Paths 1 and 2)

     - Group 3: Paths passing through E (Paths 5 and 6)
     - Group 4: Paths passing through C (Paths 3, 4, 5, and 6, but subset to a direct route with B)
     - Group 5: Paths passing through P (Paths 1 and 2)

4. **Determine the cut set:**
   To disconnect H and O, we need to remove at least one node from each disjoint path group that connects H and O. The nodes I and C appear in multiple paths and are common points:

   - Removing I will break paths 1 and 2.
   - Removing C will break paths 3, 4, 5, and 6.
   - Removing P will break alternate paths via P (redundant as removing I or C already covers these).
   - Removing B will overlap with node C removal. Then focus if alternate paths are accounted per above.

Therefore, to ensure no path remains between H and O, we can remove nodes I and C.

**Final Answer:** Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [I, C].

7. B → O → Y → H → K → F → G → H → I → P → E → M

9. B → E → L → J → P → E → M

Key edges to consider that, when removed, would break multiple paths:
a. (E, M) would cut paths that go through E → M directly.
b. (E, J) would cut paths that traverse through E → J.
c. (B, E) would cut paths that traverse through B → E.

- If we remove (E, J), paths involving E-J are cut. This ensures that paths breaking through (D, M) and (P, M) are cut when involving E-J.

- (B → E → M) is cut by removing (E, M).
- (B → P → E → M) is cut by removing (B, E).
- All paths involving E-J are cut.
- Since (B, E) is cut, paths involving B → E → other paths toward M are effectively cut off.

Thus, the minimum set of edges to remove is:

   - Path 5: J -> P -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F

     - Group 1: Paths that go through node M.
     - Group 2: Paths that go through node H.
   - Removing either M or H alone will not disconnect J and F because there are alternative paths.

4. **Determine the minimum cut set:**
   - To disconnect J and F, we need to remove nodes that are part of all paths.
   - Removing M will block Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3.
   - Removing either H or any crucial intermediary nodes such as K can further block the other path.

   - Nodes: H and either M or K.

   - Removing H disconnects the right side of the graph, blocking all paths through H.
   - Removing M disconnects the left side of the graph, blocking all paths through M.
   - Removing either K handles the connectivity the last paths

6. **Other combinations and nodes validation:**
   - After contextual verification by visual observation (to ensure all existing paths between two target nodes are abolished). I confirm minimalcut-centrality validating these.

     - D-J-P-O-N-B-E-F

     - D-J-P-O-N-B-E-L (then back to F through E as they share edge)
     - D-J-P-O-Y-C-F
     - D-J-P-O-N-C-F
     - D-J-P-Y-N-C-F

3. **Cutting Edges:**
   - **Edge (J, P):** Cutting this edge removes the primary connection between D and J.
   - **Edge (N, B):** Cutting this edge removes the primary connection between N and B for both paths.
   - **Edge (B, E):** Cutting this edge removes the primary connection between B and F.

### Verification:
- **After removing (J, P):** D is now isolated from the rest of the graph.
- **After removing (N, B):** Any path connecting from N to B is now broken. Therefore, paths through N-B are removed.
- **Edge (B, E):** Ensures that F and any nodes going through B to reach F are disconnected.

### Final Cut Set:
- Removing the edges (J, P), (N, B), (B, E) ensures that there is no path between D and F.

### Alternative Consideration:
- Removing (D, J) directly isolates D, but we must ensure all alternative paths connecting F.
- Removing (F, E) directly isolates F.
- Cutting (N, B) removes both edges.

### Conclusion:
Edge removal set [(J, P), (N, B), (B, E)] is the smallest possible minimal cut set of size 3 that ensures complete disconnection.

**Final Answer:** Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, P), (N, B), (B, E)]

     - B → H
   - **Indirect Paths**:
     - B → C → N → O → P → J → I → E → G → F → K → X → G → H
     - B → C → N → O → P → J → I → E → G → H

     - B → C → N → O → P → J → I → E → F → K → G → H
     - B → C → N → O → P → J → I → E → G → F → X → G → H

   - Removing the direct edge (B, H) will break the direct connection.
   - To ensure full disconnection, each of the alternative paths must also be broken. Since multiple paths converge at E and G, focusing on these nodes helps.

4. **Verify critical edges removal**:

   - Remove (C, N): This cuts off the path through B → C → N.
